In a chic space with black walls and a ceiling adorned in gold, you can enjoy tempura with a light and elegant texture, delicately fried using refined techniques to achieve a thin, white batter that minimizes the aroma of oil. Savor tempura that maximizes the flavors of the ingredients, paired with Champagne and Burgundy wines carefully selected by the sommelier.\n\n    View on JapanEatinerary\n  + \n\n    Izakaya [...] Sign In\n\n# Alcoholic Beverages\n\nDrinking plays an important role in Japanese society. Drinking parties, typically held at restaurants and izakaya, are a common activity that are used to strengthen both social and business ties. A large variety of alcoholic beverages can be found in Japan. Some of the most popular ones are:\n\n \n\n  Beer and beer-like drinks\n\n  Beer is the most popular alcoholic drink in Japan. To qualify for lower tax rates, breweries developed beer-like beverages such as happoshu and new-genre beer, using less malt or alternative ingredients. The lower taxes allowed these drinks to be sold at lower prices, but their tax advantage was abolished in 2026.\n \n\n  Sake (rice wine) [...] Highball\n\n  Whisky highball, often simply called highball, is a carbonated drink made of whisky and soda water. Originally popularized in the 1950s, the drink has enjoyed a resurgence as it has been successfully promoted as an alternative to beer. Highball has an alcohol content of 5-10 percent and is widely available at restaurants and elsewhere.\n \n\n  Chuhai\n\n  Chuhai (shortened from \"shochu highball\") are fruit-flavored alcoholic drinks with an alcohol content of 3-8 percent. Common flavors include lemon, ume, peach, grapefruit and lime in addition to seasonal flavors. Chuhai are made of shochu and soda, and are available premixed in cans anywhere alcohol is sold.\n \n\n  Plum wine (umeshu)",
      "score": 0.23672834,
      "raw_content": "[Sign In](/login/?aACTION=url&aURL=/e/e2037.html )\n\n\n\n\n# Alcoholic Beverages\n\nDrinking plays an important role in Japanese society. Drinking parties, typically held at [restaurants](e2036.html) and [izakaya](e2036_izakaya.html), are a common activity that are used to strengthen both social and business ties. A large variety of alcoholic beverages can be found in Japan. Some of the most popular ones are:\n\n* ![]()\n\n  [Beer and beer-like drinks](e2037_beer.html)\n\n  [Beer](e2037_beer.html) is the most popular alcoholic drink in Japan. To qualify for lower tax rates, breweries developed beer-like beverages such as happoshu and new-genre beer, using less malt or alternative ingredients. The lower taxes allowed these drinks to be sold at lower prices, but their tax advantage was abolished in 2026.\n* ![]()\n\n  [Sake (rice wine)](e2037_sake.html)\n\n  Commonly called [sake](e2037_sake.html) outside of Japan, nihonshu or sake (note that \"sake\" is also the general Japanese term for alcohol) is brewed using [rice](e2043.html), water and koji mold as the main ingredients. Besides major brands, there are countless local rice wines (jizake). The alcohol content of nihonshu is typically about 10-20 percent. It is drunk hot, at room temperature or cold, and it is usually filtered although unfiltered nihonshu is also available.\n* ![]()\n\n  Shochu and awamori\n\n  Shochu is a distilled spirit with an alcohol content of 20-40 percent. It is commonly made from [rice](e2043.html), sweet potatoes, wheat and/or sugar cane. It is usually served mixed with water and ice, fruit juice and sparkling water, or [oolong tea](e2041.html). Shochu is popular nationwide, but particularly so in [Kyushu](/list/e1108.html). Awamori is the [Okinawan](/list/e1247.html) version of shochu. It differs in that it is made from long-grained Thai-style rice instead of short-grained Japanese-style rice and uses a black koji mold indigenous to Okinawa.\n* ![]()\n\n  [Whisky](e2037_whisky.html)\n\n  Despite their comparably short history, [Japanese whiskies](e2037_whisky.html) are now on par with some of the finest Scotch whiskies and have won top international awards. They are currently enjoying high popularity inside and outside of Japan.\n* ![]()\n\n  Highball\n\n  Whisky highball, often simply called highball, is a carbonated drink made of [whisky](e2037_whisky.html) and soda water. Originally popularized in the 1950s, the drink has enjoyed a resurgence as it has been successfully promoted as an alternative to beer. Highball has an alcohol content of 5-10 percent and is widely available at restaurants and elsewhere.\n* ![]()\n\n  Chuhai\n\n  Chuhai (shortened from \"shochu highball\") are fruit-flavored alcoholic drinks with an alcohol content of 3-8 percent. Common flavors include lemon, [ume](e2013.html), peach, grapefruit and lime in addition to seasonal flavors. Chuhai are made of shochu and soda, and are available premixed in cans anywhere alcohol is sold.\n* ![]()\n\n  Plum wine (umeshu)\n\n  Umeshu is made of [Japanese plums (ume)](e2013.html), sugar, and shochu or nihonshu. Its sweet, fruity, juice-like flavor and aroma can appeal to those who normally dislike alcohol. Umeshu is easily found anywhere alcohol is sold and is usually served on the rocks, mixed with soda, or as an umeshu sawa (umeshu sour).\n* ![]()\n\n  Wine\n\n  Wine has gradually been gaining popularity in Japan over the decades. While imported red, white and sparkling wines from France, Italy, the United States, Chile and Australia are widely available, there also exists a sizable and increasing domestic wine industry. The most famous wine producing region within Japan is the [Kofu basin](e6954.html) in [Yamanashi Prefecture](/list/e1217.html).\n\nAlcoholic beverages are sold in [supermarkets](e2073.html), [department stores](e2072.html), [convenience stores](e2071.html), liquor stores (saka-ya) and a decreasing number of [vending machines](e2010.html). The legal drinking age is 20 years old, the same as for purchasing [tobacco](e2228.html) products.\n\n### Drinking manners\n\nWhen drinking alcoholic beverages, it is customary to serve one another rather than serving yourself. Periodically check your friends' glasses and replenish them before they get empty. Likewise, if someone wants to serve you, take a sip to make some room in your glass and hold it up for the person while they pour. Then take one sip before putting the glass down.\n\nAt the beginning of a meal or drinking party you should not start drinking until everybody at the table is served and the glasses are raised for a toast, which is usually \"kampai\". Other toasts are acceptable, too, but note that \"chin chin\" refers to the male genitalia in [Japanese](e621.html) and may not be the ideal word choice depending on the situation.\n\nWhile it is considered bad manners to become obviously drunk in formal [restaurants](e2036.html), the same is not true at informal restaurants, such as [izakaya](e2036_izakaya.html), as long as you do not bother other guests.\n\n**Questions?** Ask in our [forum](/forum/quedisplay.html).\n\n## Restaurants\n\nRecommended in Japan\n\n[powered by![](/ad/banner/omakase.png)](https://omakaseje.com/?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=links-and-resources&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n* + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/cv127994?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Udatsu Sushi (Tokyo)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/cv127994?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Sushi\n\n    Awarded One Star in 2024 - People from around the world visit to experience Mr. Udatsu's sushi. Inside the restaurant, which resembles an art gallery with its modern decor and numerous artworks, guests can enjoy sushi crafted from the highest quality ingredients. While the foundation is traditional nigiri, the menu also features original creations born from the chef's relentless curiosity and innovation.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/cv127994?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/wk885579?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Waketokuyama (Tokyo)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/wk885579?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Japanese (Kaiseki)\n\n    Awarded One Star in 2025 - With a meticulous focus on allowing guests to enjoy seasonal ingredients at their peak, the menu changes approximately every two weeks. The signature dish, \"Grilled Abalone with Seaweed Aroma,\" features thick slices of abalone generously coated in a rich liver sauce, offering an exquisite taste of the sea.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/wk885579?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/gk119166?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Sushiroku (Osaka)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/gk119166?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Sushi\n\n    Awarded One Star in 2024 - A cozy, family-run restaurant managed by a husband and wife. They are deeply committed to perfecting their shari (sushi rice) and use two types of vinegared rice tailored to complement each topping. Since 2019, the restaurant has consistently earned stars.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/gk119166?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/cx206770?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Fry-ya (Tokyo)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/cx206770?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Tonkatsu\n\n    Exquisite fried dishes crafted by a head chef with experience earning stars in both Switzerland and Japan. The remarkably light tonkatsu is a favorite not only among Japanese diners but also among visitors to Japan. With the theme of \"small portions, many varieties,\" guests can enjoy sampling a wide selection of tonkatsu in smaller portions.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/cx206770?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/qn632134?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Sushi Hayashi (Kyoto)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/qn632134?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Sushi\n\n    Awarded One Star in 2024 - A unique sushi restaurant that blends traditional Edomae (Tokyo-style) sushi with Kyoto-style sushi, such as mackerel sushi and steamed sushi, in its courses. The head chef, who trained as a sushi artisan in Switzerland, carefully selects Swiss wines, making them a perfect pairing to enjoy with the meal.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/qn632134?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/xg289172?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Hikarimono (Tokyo)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/xg289172?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Sushi\n\n    With a prime location and quality that rivals high-end sushi restaurants, this restaurant maintains the goal of being a place for everyday dining. It offers a casual and relaxed atmosphere, free from stiffness or formality. The signature \"Hikari-maki,\" featuring ingredients such as sardines, pickled plum, and bettarazuke (sweet pickled radish), boasts unique flavors that are especially popular among international visitors.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/xg289172?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ja444503?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Noguchi Tsunagu (Kyoto)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ja444503?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Japanese (Kaiseki)\n\n    Awarded One Star in 2024 - The sister restaurant of the highly exclusive Japanese cuisine establishment, Kyotenjin Noguchi. While maintaining the culinary essence of the main branch, this kappo-style restaurant incorporates ingredients from the chef’s hometown in the Goto Islands. Its signature dish, Nikusui, is a masterpiece made from carefully prepared, top-quality A5-grade sirloin.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ja444503?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ui464102?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [TEMPURA & WINE SHINO (Tokyo)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ui464102?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Tempura\n\n    The kind of restaurant that is known only to true gourmets, serving as a sort of 'Hidden gem'. In a chic space with black walls and a ceiling adorned in gold, you can enjoy tempura with a light and elegant texture, delicately fried using refined techniques to achieve a thin, white batter that minimizes the aroma of oil. Savor tempura that maximizes the flavors of the ingredients, paired with Champagne and Burgundy wines carefully selected by the sommelier.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ui464102?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/zw312704?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Ginza Nominokoji Yamagishi (Tokyo)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/zw312704?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Izakaya\n\n    Tominokoji Yamagishi, an exclusive kaiseki restaurant from Kyoto, has opened its first location in Tokyo. Unlike its main branch, this establishment adopts an izakaya-style format, allowing diners to enjoy a more relaxed à la carte dining experience. Despite being located in Tokyo, the restaurant meticulously sources ingredients and even water from Kyoto, dedicating itself to faithfully recreating Kyoto’s culinary traditions.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/zw312704?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n  + [![]()](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ug953416?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    [Kitashinchi Kushikatsu Bon (Osaka)](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ug953416?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n    Kushikatsu\n\n    A restaurant that elevates Osaka's soul food, kushikatsu, to a luxurious level. Skilled chefs meticulously prepare each skewer using carefully selected premium ingredients such as Chateaubriand and foie gras. The skewers are fried in a custom copper pot using a unique oil blend based on cottonseed oil, enhancing the natural flavors of the ingredients.\n\n    [View on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants/ug953416?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_restaurant&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n  [See more on JapanEatinerary](https://omakaseje.com/restaurants?rpuid=PTR_mGR15LVDA0SbNzO0I3FK&utm_content=footer_widget_seemore&utm_medium=referral&utm_source=japan-guide)\n\n## Experiences\n\n* + [![]()\n\n    Sponsored](/link.html?/e/e2037.html+activity+wabunka+1+23+sponsored+https://wabunka-lux.jp/experiences/en_nagai-shuzo/xQUErefxEQpluvcusc)\n\n    [Taste Traditional and Innovative Sake with a Terroir Tour of Nagai Sake Brewery in Kawaba Village, Gunma](/link.html?/e/e2037.html+activity+wabunka+1+23+sponsored+https://wabunka-lux.jp/experiences/en_nagai-shuzo/xQUErefxEQpluvcusc)\n\n    Nagai Sake Brewery was founded in 1886 in Gunma Prefecturefs Kawaba Village, a locale brimming with natural beauty. In this experience, tour the breweryfs terroir – the natural environment whose distinctive features all factor into the flavor of its sake. Enjoy a toast at the source of the natural waters which are crucial to the sakefs brewing process, and a tour of the fields of rice from the townfs special brand of Yukinhotaka rice is grown. Finally, enjoy a tasting of three signature brands of Nagai sake at their special gSHINKAh tasting room.\n\n    [View experience](/link.html?/e/e2037.html+activity+wabunka+1+23+sponsored+https://wabunka-lux.jp/experiences/en_nagai-shuzo/xQUErefxEQpluvcusc)\n\n    Sponsored\n\nAnything we can improve?  \n\n**Thank you for your feedback.**\n\nPage last updated:\n\n ",
